\maketitle
\noindent This document contains all 70 \emph{Definite Integration} questions, each with a
fully worked solution and a TikZ graph. A definite integral measures the signed area between the curve and the
\(x\)-axis over the given limits, so each diagram shades that region: \textcolor{green!55!black}{green} where the
curve is above the axis and \textcolor{orange!80!black}{orange} where it is below (illustrating signed vs total
area), with \textcolor{blue!60!black}{blue} for the area trapped between two curves. The integration limits are
shown as dashed lines.

\section*{Definite Integration 01\quad\small\texttt{(i2-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 01 (i2-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^3 (2x + 5)\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate term by term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (2x + 5)\, \mathrm{d}x = x^2 + 5x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule to each term. No \( +C \) is needed for a definite integral --- if we wrote \( F(x) + C \) then the \( +C \) would cancel in the subtraction \( F(b) - F(a) \), so it never affects the answer.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the upper limit \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3)^2 + 5(3) = 9 + 15 = 24
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 3 \) into the integrated expression.

\textit{Step 3: Apply the lower limit \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(1)^2 + 5(1) = 1 + 5 = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \) into the integrated expression.

\textit{Step 4: Subtract lower from upper.}
\[\begin{gathered}
24 - 6 = 18
\end{gathered}\]
The definite integral equals \( F(b) - F(a) \), upper minus lower. Reversing the order would flip the sign, so always subtract the lower-limit value from the upper-limit value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 18\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 02\quad\small\texttt{(i2-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 02 (i2-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^2 (3x^2 - 4x + 1)\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate term by term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (3x^2 - 4x + 1)\, \mathrm{d}x = x^3 - 2x^2 + x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule to each term.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2)^3 - 2(2)^2 + 2 = 8 - 8 + 2 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit substitution.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 - 0 + 0 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit substitution gives zero.

\textit{Step 4: Subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2 - 0 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The value of the definite integral.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Definite Integration 03\quad\small\texttt{(i2-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 03 (i2-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 \sqrt{x}\, \mathrm{d}x \), giving an exact answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2} \) and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int x^{1/2}\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^{3/2}}{3/2} = \dfrac{2}{3}x^{3/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Use the power rule with \( n = \tfrac{1}{2} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{2}{3}(4)^{3/2} = \dfrac{2}{3} \times 8 = \dfrac{16}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^{3/2} = (\sqrt{4})^3 = 2^3 = 8 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{2}{3}(1)^{3/2} = \dfrac{2}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 1^{3/2} = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{16}{3} - \dfrac{2}{3} = \dfrac{14}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact value of the integral.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{14}{3}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 04\quad\small\texttt{(i2-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 04 (i2-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^2 \dfrac{4}{x^2}\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite \( \dfrac{4}{x^2} = 4x^{-2} \) and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int 4x^{-2}\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{4x^{-1}}{-1} = -\dfrac{4}{x}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule with \( n = -2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-\dfrac{4}{2} = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit substitution.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-\dfrac{4}{1} = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit substitution.

\textit{Step 4: Subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2 - (-4) = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Upper minus lower limit value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 05\quad\small\texttt{(i2-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 05 (i2-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_{-1}^{2} (x^2 + 3)\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^2 + 3)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^3}{3} + 3x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule applied to each term.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{8}{3} + 6 = \dfrac{8}{3} + \dfrac{18}{3} = \dfrac{26}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit gives \( \tfrac{26}{3} \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{(-1)^3}{3} + 3(-1) = -\dfrac{1}{3} - 3 = -\dfrac{10}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Take care with negatives when cubing.

\textit{Step 4: Subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{26}{3} - \left(-\dfrac{10}{3}\right) = \dfrac{36}{3} = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting a negative adds the value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 12\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 06\quad\small\texttt{(i2-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 06 (i2-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^3 (4x^3 - 6x)\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (4x^3 - 6x)\, \mathrm{d}x = x^4 - 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule to each term.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3)^4 - 3(3)^2 = 81 - 27 = 54
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit substitution.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 - 0 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit substitution.

\textit{Step 4: Subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
54 - 0 = 54
\end{gathered}\]
Value of the definite integral.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 54\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 07\quad\small\texttt{(i2-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 07 (i2-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 \left(x + \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\right)\mathrm{d}x \), giving an exact answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite \( \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{x}} = x^{-1/2} \) and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int \left(x + x^{-1/2}\right)\mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^2}{2} + 2x^{1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \int x^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^{1/2}}{1/2} = 2\sqrt{x} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{16}{2} + 2\sqrt{4} = 8 + 4 = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit substitution.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{1}{2} + 2\sqrt{1} = \dfrac{1}{2} + 2 = \dfrac{5}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit substitution.

\textit{Step 4: Subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12 - \dfrac{5}{2} = \dfrac{19}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact answer as a fraction.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{19}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 08\quad\small\texttt{(i2-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 08 (i2-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{By first expanding, evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^3 (x+2)(x-1)\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand the brackets.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+2)(x-1) = x^2 + x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply out before integrating.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^2 + x - 2)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^3}{3} + \dfrac{x^2}{2} - 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule on each term.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
9 + \dfrac{9}{2} - 6 = 3 + \dfrac{9}{2} = \dfrac{15}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \dfrac{27}{3} + \dfrac{9}{2} - 6 = 9 + 4.5 - 6 = 7.5 \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( x = 0 \) and subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{15}{2} - 0 = \dfrac{15}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit gives zero.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{15}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 09\quad\small\texttt{(i2-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 09 (i2-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{By first expanding, evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^2 x^2(2x - 3)\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2(2x-3) = 2x^3 - 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply each term inside the bracket by \( x^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (2x^3 - 3x^2)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^4}{2} - x^3
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule on each term.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{16}{2} - 8 = 8 - 8 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit substitution.

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( x = 1 \) and subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{1}{2} - 1 = -\dfrac{1}{2} \implies 0 - \left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\right) = \dfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting a negative value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{1}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 10\quad\small\texttt{(i2-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 10 (i2-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^3 (2x+1)^2\, \mathrm{d}x \) by first expanding the bracket.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand \( (2x+1)^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2x+1)^2 = 4x^2 + 4x + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( (a+b)^2 = a^2 + 2ab + b^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (4x^2 + 4x + 1)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{4x^3}{3} + 2x^2 + x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule on each term.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{4(27)}{3} + 2(9) + 3 = 36 + 18 + 3 = 57
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit substitution.

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( x = 1 \) and subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{4}{3} + 2 + 1 = \dfrac{13}{3} \implies 57 - \dfrac{13}{3} = \dfrac{171 - 13}{3} = \dfrac{158}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract to find the definite integral.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{158}{3}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 11\quad\small\texttt{(i2-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 11 (i2-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the value of \( a \), where \( a > 0 \), given that \( \displaystyle\int_0^a (6x^2 + 2)\, \mathrm{d}x = 20 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (6x^2 + 2)\, \mathrm{d}x = 2x^3 + 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule term by term; treat \( a \) as a numerical upper limit. No \( +C \) is needed here because the constant cancels in \( F(a) - F(0) \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[2x^3 + 2x\right]_0^a = (2a^3 + 2a) - 0 = 2a^3 + 2a
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute the upper limit \( x = a \) and then the lower limit \( x = 0 \) and subtract. The lower limit contributes zero.

\textit{Step 3: Set equal to 20 and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2a^3 + 2a = 20 \implies a^3 + a = 10
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 2 to give a cleaner cubic in \( a \).

\textit{Step 4: Solve by inspection.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 2: \; 2^3 + 2 = 8 + 2 = 10 \;\checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
Test small positive integers. \( a = 2 \) satisfies \( a^3 + a = 10 \), so \( a = 2 \). Since \( a > 0 \) is required and \( a^3 + a \) is strictly increasing, this is the only valid solution.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle a = 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 12\quad\small\texttt{(i2-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 12 (i2-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the value of \( a \), where \( a > 0 \), given that \( \displaystyle\int_0^a (4x + 3)\, \mathrm{d}x = 22 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (4x + 3)\, \mathrm{d}x = 2x^2 + 3x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule on each term.

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[2x^2 + 3x\right]_0^a = 2a^2 + 3a
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit gives zero.

\textit{Step 3: Set equal to 22.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2a^2 + 3a = 22 \implies 2a^2 + 3a - 22 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange to a standard quadratic.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise and solve, taking \( a > 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2a + 11)(a - 2) = 0 \implies a = 2 \text{ (since } a > 0)
\end{gathered}\]
\( a = -\tfrac{11}{2} \) is rejected as \( a > 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle a = 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 13\quad\small\texttt{(i2-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 13 (i2-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the possible values of \( k \) that satisfy \( \displaystyle\int_1^3 (kx + 2)\, \mathrm{d}x = 12 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate, treating \( k \) as a constant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (kx + 2)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{kx^2}{2} + 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Treat \( k \) as a numerical constant throughout.

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{kx^2}{2} + 2x\right]_1^3 = \left(\dfrac{9k}{2} + 6\right) - \left(\dfrac{k}{2} + 2\right) = 4k + 4
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract upper minus lower.

\textit{Step 3: Set equal to 12.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4k + 4 = 12 \implies 4k = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Simple linear equation in \( k \).

\textit{Step 4: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Unique value of \( k \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle k = 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 14\quad\small\texttt{(i2-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 14 (i2-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the possible values of \( A \) that satisfy \( \displaystyle\int_0^2 (x^2 + Ax)\, \mathrm{d}x = 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^2 + Ax)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^3}{3} + \dfrac{Ax^2}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Treat \( A \) as a constant.

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits 0 to 2.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{x^3}{3} + \dfrac{Ax^2}{2}\right]_0^2 = \dfrac{8}{3} + 2A - 0
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit gives zero.

\textit{Step 3: Set equal to 6.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{8}{3} + 2A = 6 \implies 2A = 6 - \dfrac{8}{3} = \dfrac{10}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange for \( A \).

\textit{Step 4: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = \dfrac{5}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 2.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle A = \dfrac{5}{3}\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 15\quad\small\texttt{(i2-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 15 (i2-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find all possible values of \( A \) satisfying \( \displaystyle\int_0^3 (x^2 + 2Ax + A^2)\, \mathrm{d}x = 30 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^2 + 2Ax + A^2)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^3}{3} + Ax^2 + A^2 x
\end{gathered}\]
Treat \( A \) as a constant.

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits 0 to 3.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{x^3}{3} + Ax^2 + A^2 x\right]_0^3 = 9 + 9A + 3A^2
\end{gathered}\]
\( \tfrac{27}{3} + 9A + 3A^2 = 9 + 9A + 3A^2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Set equal to 30 and rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
9 + 9A + 3A^2 = 30 \implies 3A^2 + 9A - 21 = 0 \implies A^2 + 3A - 7 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide through by 3.

\textit{Step 4: Use the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = \dfrac{-3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 28}}{2} = \dfrac{-3 \pm \sqrt{37}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Both values are valid solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle A = \dfrac{-3 + \sqrt{37}}{2}\) or \(\displaystyle A = \dfrac{-3 - \sqrt{37}}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\end{center}
\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 16\quad\small\texttt{(i2-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 16 (i2-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the area enclosed between the curve \( y = x^2 \), the \( x \)-axis and the lines \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up the definite integral for the area.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \int_0^3 x^2\, \mathrm{d}x
\end{gathered}\]
The curve lies above the \( x \)-axis for \( x \geq 0 \), so the integral gives the area directly.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int x^2\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^3}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^3 = \dfrac{27}{3} - 0 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit minus lower limit.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 9\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 17\quad\small\texttt{(i2-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 17 (i2-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the exact area enclosed between the curve \( y = 3x^2 + 1 \), the \( x \)-axis and the lines \( x = 1 \) and \( x = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up the integral.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \int_1^4 (3x^2 + 1)\, \mathrm{d}x
\end{gathered}\]
The function is positive throughout this interval.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (3x^2 + 1)\, \mathrm{d}x = x^3 + x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[x^3 + x\right]_1^4 = (64 + 4) - (1 + 1) = 68 - 2 = 66
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate at 4 and subtract evaluation at 1.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 66\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 18\quad\small\texttt{(i2-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 18 (i2-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the area enclosed between the curve \( y = \sqrt{x} \), the \( x \)-axis and the lines \( x = 1 \) and \( x = 9 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2} \) and set up the integral.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \int_1^9 x^{1/2}\, \mathrm{d}x
\end{gathered}\]
The function is positive for \( x > 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int x^{1/2}\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{2}{3}x^{3/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule with \( n = \tfrac{1}{2} \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{2}{3}x^{3/2}\right]_1^9 = \dfrac{2}{3}(27) - \dfrac{2}{3}(1) = 18 - \dfrac{2}{3} = \dfrac{52}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 9^{3/2} = (\sqrt{9})^3 = 27 \); \( 1^{3/2} = 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{52}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 19\quad\small\texttt{(i2-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 19 (i2-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( y = x^2 - 4 \) intersects the \( x \)-axis at \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 2 \). Find the area enclosed between the curve and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Note that \( y = x^2 - 4 \leq 0 \) between \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 2 \), so the integral will be negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-2}^{2}(x^2 - 4)\, \mathrm{d}x
\end{gathered}\]
Area below the \( x \)-axis must be made positive at the end.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^2 - 4)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^3}{3} - 4x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{x^3}{3} - 4x\right]_{-2}^{2} = \left(\dfrac{8}{3} - 8\right) - \left(-\dfrac{8}{3} + 8\right) = -\dfrac{16}{3} - \dfrac{16}{3} = -\dfrac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate at both limits and subtract.

\textit{Step 4: Make the area positive.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \left|-\dfrac{32}{3}\right| = \dfrac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Area is always positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{32}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 20\quad\small\texttt{(i2-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 20 (i2-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the area enclosed by the curve \( y = (x-1)(x-3) \) and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find where the curve crosses the \( x \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x-1)(x-3) = 0 \implies x = 1 \text{ and } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The curve dips below the \( x \)-axis between these roots.

\textit{Step 2: Expand and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_1^3 (x^2 - 4x + 3)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^3}{3} - 2x^2 + 3x\right]_1^3
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the product before integrating.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(9 - 18 + 9) - \left(\dfrac{1}{3} - 2 + 3\right) = 0 - \dfrac{4}{3} = -\dfrac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
The integral is negative as the curve is below the \( x \)-axis.

\textit{Step 4: Take the absolute value.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \dfrac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Area must be positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{4}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 21\quad\small\texttt{(i2-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 21 (i2-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area enclosed by the curve \( y = x(x-2)(x+1) \) and the \( x \)-axis between \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand \( y = x(x-2)(x+1) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(x-2)(x+1) = x(x^2 - x - 2) = x^3 - x^2 - 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Expand step by step.

\textit{Step 2: Note that for \( 0 \leq x \leq 2 \) the curve is at or below the \( x \)-axis (it crosses at \( x=0 \) and \( x=2 \)).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^2 (x^3 - x^2 - 2x)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^4}{4} - \dfrac{x^3}{3} - x^2\right]_0^2
\end{gathered}\]
The integral will be negative; take the absolute value for area.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(4 - \dfrac{8}{3} - 4\right) - 0 = -\dfrac{8}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit gives \( 4 - \tfrac{8}{3} - 4 = -\tfrac{8}{3} \).

\textit{Step 4: Take the absolute value.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \dfrac{8}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Area is positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{8}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 22\quad\small\texttt{(i2-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 22 (i2-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the total area between the graph of \( y = x^3 - x \) and the \( x \)-axis between \( x = -1 \) and \( x = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find roots of \( y = x^3 - x = x(x-1)(x+1) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -1,\; 0,\; 1
\end{gathered}\]
The curve changes sign at each root --- split the integral.

\textit{Step 2: Area from \( -1 \) to \( 0 \) (curve above \( x \)-axis here).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-1}^{0}(x^3 - x)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^4}{4} - \dfrac{x^2}{2}\right]_{-1}^{0} = 0 - \left(\dfrac{1}{4} - \dfrac{1}{2}\right) = \dfrac{1}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
Positive area here.

\textit{Step 3: Area from \( 0 \) to \( 1 \) (curve below \( x \)-axis).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{0}^{1}(x^3 - x)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^4}{4} - \dfrac{x^2}{2}\right]_0^1 = \dfrac{1}{4} - \dfrac{1}{2} = -\dfrac{1}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
Negative, so area is \( \tfrac{1}{4} \).

\textit{Step 4: Total area = sum of absolute values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{1}{4} + \dfrac{1}{4} = \dfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Add both areas (both taken as positive).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{1}{2}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 23\quad\small\texttt{(i2-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 23 (i2-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the total shaded area enclosed between the curve \( y = x^2 - 1 \) and the \( x \)-axis between \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find where the curve crosses the \( x \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 1 = 0 \implies x = \pm 1
\end{gathered}\]
The curve is below the \( x \)-axis for \( -1 < x < 1 \) and above it outside this interval.

\textit{Step 2: Area above the \( x \)-axis: \( x \in [-2,-1] \) and \( x \in [1,2] \). By symmetry, compute one piece and double.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_1^2 (x^2 - 1)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^3}{3} - x\right]_1^2 = \left(\dfrac{8}{3} - 2\right) - \left(\dfrac{1}{3} - 1\right) = \dfrac{7}{3} - 1 = \dfrac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
On \([1,2]\) the curve is above the \( x \)-axis, so the integral equals the signed area directly. By the even symmetry of \( y = x^2 - 1 \), the area from \(-2\) to \(-1\) is also \( \tfrac{4}{3} \).

\textit{Step 3: Area below the \( x \)-axis: \( x \in [-1,1] \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-1}^{1}(x^2 - 1)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^3}{3} - x\right]_{-1}^{1} = \left(\dfrac{1}{3}-1\right) - \left(-\dfrac{1}{3}+1\right) = -\dfrac{2}{3} - \dfrac{2}{3} = -\dfrac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Negative; area is \( \tfrac{4}{3} \).

\textit{Step 4: Total area.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{4}{3} + \dfrac{4}{3} + \dfrac{4}{3} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Three equal pieces of \( \tfrac{4}{3} \) each.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 4\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\end{center}
\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 24\quad\small\texttt{(i2-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 24 (i2-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the area enclosed by the curve \( y = 4 - x^2 \) and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find where the curve meets the \( x \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4 - x^2 = 0 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
The parabola is above the \( x \)-axis between \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-2}^{2}(4 - x^2)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[4x - \dfrac{x^3}{3}\right]_{-2}^{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Curve is above the \( x \)-axis here --- no sign change needed.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(8 - \dfrac{8}{3}\right) - \left(-8 + \dfrac{8}{3}\right) = \dfrac{16}{3} + \dfrac{16}{3} = \dfrac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate at 2 and \(-2\), then subtract.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{32}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 25\quad\small\texttt{(i2-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 25 (i2-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the area enclosed between the curve \( y = (x+1)(3-x) \) and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find roots: the curve meets the \( x \)-axis at \( x = -1 \) and \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+1)(3-x) = 0 \implies x = -1,\; x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Parabola opens downward --- curve is above \( x \)-axis between roots.

\textit{Step 2: Expand and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-1}^{3}(3 + 2x - x^2)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[3x + x^2 - \dfrac{x^3}{3}\right]_{-1}^{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( (x+1)(3-x) = 3 + 2x - x^2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
9 + 9 - 9 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit.

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( x = -1 \) and subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-3 + 1 + \dfrac{1}{3} = -\dfrac{5}{3} \implies 9 - \left(-\dfrac{5}{3}\right) = \dfrac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract lower from upper.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{32}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 26\quad\small\texttt{(i2-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 26 (i2-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The curve \( y = x^3 - 4x \) crosses the \( x \)-axis at \( x = 0 \), \( x = 2 \) and \( x = -2 \). Find the total area enclosed between the curve and the \( x \)-axis between \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: For \( -2 \leq x \leq 0 \), the curve is above the \( x \)-axis; for \( 0 \leq x \leq 2 \), it is below.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Split: } \int_{-2}^{0}(x^3-4x)\,\mathrm{d}x \text{ and } \int_{0}^{2}(x^3-4x)\,\mathrm{d}x
\end{gathered}\]
Use the sketch or test values to determine sign of \( y \).

\textit{Step 2: Integrate \( x^3 - 4x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^3 - 4x)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{x^4}{4} - 2x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Area from \( -2 \) to \( 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{x^4}{4} - 2x^2\right]_{-2}^{0} = 0 - (4 - 8) = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Lower limit gives \( 4 - 8 = -4 \); subtract to get \( 0 - (-4) = 4 \).

\textit{Step 4: Area from \( 0 \) to \( 2 \), take absolute value.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\dfrac{x^4}{4} - 2x^2\right]_0^2 = (4 - 8) - 0 = -4 \implies |{-4}| = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Negative result means area below \( x \)-axis; take absolute value.

\textit{Step 5: Total area.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4 + 4 = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Add the two areas.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 8\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\end{center}
\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 27\quad\small\texttt{(i2-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 27 (i2-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the exact area of the region enclosed between the curve \( y = \dfrac{1}{x^2} \), the \( x \)-axis and the lines \( x = 1 \) and \( x = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite \( \dfrac{1}{x^2} = x^{-2} \) and set up the integral.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \int_1^3 x^{-2}\, \mathrm{d}x
\end{gathered}\]
The function is positive for \( x > 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int x^{-2}\, \mathrm{d}x = -x^{-1} = -\dfrac{1}{x}
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule with \( n = -2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[-\dfrac{1}{x}\right]_1^3 = -\dfrac{1}{3} - (-1) = -\dfrac{1}{3} + 1 = \dfrac{2}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Upper limit minus lower limit.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{2}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 28\quad\small\texttt{(i2-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 28 (i2-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Integrate \( f(x) = 2x - x^2 \) between \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 1 \), and find the area enclosed between this curve and the \( x \)-axis between these limits.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Note that \( y = 2x - x^2 = x(2-x) \geq 0 \) for \( 0 \leq x \leq 2 \), so the curve is above the axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \int_0^1 (2x - x^2)\, \mathrm{d}x
\end{gathered}\]
No sign complication here.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (2x - x^2)\, \mathrm{d}x = x^2 - \dfrac{x^3}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[x^2 - \dfrac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^1 = 1 - \dfrac{1}{3} - 0 = \dfrac{2}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate at \( x = 1 \) and subtract \( x = 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{2}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 29\quad\small\texttt{(i2-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 29 (i2-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A particle moves along a straight line. Its velocity, \( v \) m/s, at time \( t \) seconds is given by \( v = 6t - t^2 \). The particle starts from rest at \( t = 0 \). \\[3pt] (a) Find the distance travelled between \( t = 0 \) and \( t = 6 \). \\[3pt] (b) Find the distance travelled between \( t = 0 \) and \( t = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find when \( v = 0 \) to identify any sign changes.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6t - t^2 = t(6 - t) = 0 \implies t = 0 \text{ or } t = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Velocity is zero (particle at rest) at these times. For \( 0 < t < 6 \), \( v > 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: (a) Distance \( t = 0 \) to \( t = 6 \): velocity is positive throughout, so integrate directly.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^6 (6t - t^2)\, \mathrm{d}t = \left[3t^2 - \dfrac{t^3}{3}\right]_0^6 = (108 - 72) - 0 = 36
\end{gathered}\]
No need to split the integral as \( v \geq 0 \) throughout.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Distance \( t = 0 \) to \( t = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^3 (6t - t^2)\, \mathrm{d}t = \left[3t^2 - \dfrac{t^3}{3}\right]_0^3 = (27 - 9) - 0 = 18
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate at \( t = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle 36\) m   (b) \(\displaystyle 18\) m
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 30\quad\small\texttt{(i2-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 30 (i2-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{A particle moves so that its velocity \( v \) m/s at time \( t \) s is given by \( v = t^2 - 5t + 4 \). \\[3pt] (a) Find the times when the particle is at rest. \\[3pt] (b) Find the total distance travelled between \( t = 0 \) and \( t = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Set \( v = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t^2 - 5t + 4 = (t-1)(t-4) = 0 \implies t = 1 \text{ and } t = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Particle is at rest at \( t = 1 \) s and \( t = 4 \) s.

\textit{Step 2: Determine sign of \( v \) on each sub-interval.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 < t < 1: v > 0;\quad 1 < t < 4: v < 0
\end{gathered}\]
The velocity changes sign at \( t = 1 \) --- must split the integral.

\textit{Step 3: Distance from \( t = 0 \) to \( t = 1 \) (moving forward).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^1 (t^2 - 5t + 4)\, \mathrm{d}t = \left[\dfrac{t^3}{3} - \dfrac{5t^2}{2} + 4t\right]_0^1 = \dfrac{1}{3} - \dfrac{5}{2} + 4 = \dfrac{11}{6}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \tfrac{1}{3} - \tfrac{5}{2} + 4 = \tfrac{2}{6} - \tfrac{15}{6} + \tfrac{24}{6} = \tfrac{11}{6} \).

\textit{Step 4: Distance from \( t = 1 \) to \( t = 4 \) (velocity negative --- take absolute value of the integral).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_1^4 (t^2 - 5t + 4)\, \mathrm{d}t = \left(\dfrac{64}{3} - 40 + 16\right) - \left(\dfrac{1}{3} - \dfrac{5}{2} + 4\right) = -\dfrac{8}{3} - \dfrac{11}{6} = -\dfrac{27}{6} = -\dfrac{9}{2} \implies \text{distance} = \dfrac{9}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Because \( v < 0 \) on this interval, the integral is negative --- it gives signed displacement, not distance. The actual distance travelled is the absolute value, \( \tfrac{9}{2} \) m.

\textit{Step 5: Total distance.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{11}{6} + \dfrac{9}{2} = \dfrac{11}{6} + \dfrac{27}{6} = \dfrac{38}{6} = \dfrac{19}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Add the two distances.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle t = 1\) s and \(\displaystyle t = 4\) s   (b) \(\displaystyle \dfrac{19}{3}\) m
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 31\quad\small\texttt{(i2-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 31 (i2-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_4^9 \dfrac{x - \sqrt{x}}{\sqrt{x}}\, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide each term of the numerator by \( \sqrt{x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{x - \sqrt{x}}{\sqrt{x}} = \dfrac{x}{x^{1/2}} - \dfrac{x^{1/2}}{x^{1/2}} = x^{1/2} - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify before integrating.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^{1/2} - 1)\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{2}{3}x^{3/2} - x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule for \( x^{1/2} \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 9 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{2}{3}(27) - 9 = 18 - 9 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
\( 9^{3/2} = 27 \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( x = 4 \) and subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{2}{3}(8) - 4 = \dfrac{16}{3} - 4 = \dfrac{4}{3} \implies 9 - \dfrac{4}{3} = \dfrac{23}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^{3/2} = 8 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{23}{3}\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 32\quad\small\texttt{(i2-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 32 (i2-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 \dfrac{3x^2 + 2}{\sqrt{x}}\, \mathrm{d}x \), giving an exact answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide each term by \( x^{1/2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{3x^2 + 2}{x^{1/2}} = 3x^{3/2} + 2x^{-1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite using index laws before integrating.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (3x^{3/2} + 2x^{-1/2})\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{3x^{5/2}}{5/2} + \dfrac{2x^{1/2}}{1/2} = \dfrac{6}{5}x^{5/2} + 4x^{1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule on each term.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{6}{5}(32) + 4(2) = \dfrac{192}{5} + 8 = \dfrac{232}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^{5/2} = (\sqrt{4})^5 = 2^5 = 32 \); \( 4^{1/2} = 2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( x = 1 \) and subtract.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{6}{5}(1) + 4(1) = \dfrac{6}{5} + 4 = \dfrac{26}{5} \implies \dfrac{232}{5} - \dfrac{26}{5} = \dfrac{206}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 1^{5/2} = 1 \) and \( 1^{1/2} = 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{206}{5}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 33\quad\small\texttt{(i2-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 33 (i2-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The diagram shows the curve \( y = x^2 - 2x \) and the line \( y = 0 \). \\[3pt] (a) Find the coordinates of the points where the curve meets the \( x \)-axis. \\[3pt] (b) Hence find the area enclosed between the curve and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Set \( y = 0 \) and factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x = x(x - 2) = 0 \implies x = 0 \text{ and } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Points: \( (0,0) \) and \( (2,0) \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Note the curve lies below the \( x \)-axis between the roots. Set up the integral.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^2 (x^2 - 2x)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^3}{3} - x^2\right]_0^2
\end{gathered}\]
Integrate and apply limits.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(\dfrac{8}{3} - 4\right) - 0 = \dfrac{8}{3} - \dfrac{12}{3} = -\dfrac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Negative result confirms curve is below the \( x \)-axis.

\textit{Step 4: Take the absolute value.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \left|-\dfrac{4}{3}\right| = \dfrac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Area is always positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle (0,0)\) and \(\displaystyle (2,0)\)   (b) \(\displaystyle \dfrac{4}{3}\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 34\quad\small\texttt{(i2-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 34 (i2-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = 3x^2 - 12x \) and crosses the \( x \)-axis at the origin and at \( x = a \). \\[3pt] (a) Find the value of \( a \). \\[3pt] (b) Find the exact area enclosed between \( C \) and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Set \( y = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 - 12x = 3x(x - 4) = 0 \implies x = 0 \text{ or } x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
So \( a = 4 \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Note that the curve is below the \( x \)-axis for \( 0 < x < 4 \). Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^4 (3x^2 - 12x)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[x^3 - 6x^2\right]_0^4
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule on each term.

\textit{Step 3: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(64 - 96) - 0 = -32
\end{gathered}\]
Negative: the enclosed region is below the \( x \)-axis.

\textit{Step 4: Take the absolute value.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = 32
\end{gathered}\]
Area must be positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle a = 4\)   (b) \(\displaystyle 32\) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 35\quad\small\texttt{(i2-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 35 (i2-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 10}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x \). \\[3pt] (a) Show that \( C \) touches the \( x \)-axis at \( x = 3 \) and crosses it at \( x = 0 \). \\[3pt] (b) Find the area enclosed between \( C \) and the \( x \)-axis between \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 3 \). \\[3pt] (c) An object moves along a line with velocity \( v = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x \) m/s at time \( x \) s. Explain why the object does not change direction between \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 3 \), and find the total distance travelled in this interval.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Factorise \( y = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x(x^2 - 6x + 9) = x(x-3)^2
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^2 - 6x + 9 = (x-3)^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Show the behaviour at each root.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0: \text{ single root, so } C \text{ crosses the } x\text{-axis.}\newline x = 3: \text{ repeated root, so } C \text{ touches the } x\text{-axis.}
\end{gathered}\]
A double (repeated) root means the curve just touches the axis --- it does not cross.

\textit{Step 3: (b) The curve is above the \( x \)-axis for \( 0 < x < 3 \) since \( y = x(x-3)^2 \geq 0 \). Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^3 x(x-3)^2\, \mathrm{d}x = \int_0^3 (x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x)\, \mathrm{d}x = \left[\dfrac{x^4}{4} - 2x^3 + \dfrac{9x^2}{2}\right]_0^3
\end{gathered}\]
Expand then integrate.

\textit{Step 4: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(\dfrac{81}{4} - 54 + \dfrac{81}{2}\right) - 0 = \dfrac{81}{4} + \dfrac{162}{4} - \dfrac{216}{4} = \dfrac{27}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \tfrac{81}{4} - 54 + \tfrac{81}{2} = \tfrac{81 + 162 - 216}{4} = \tfrac{27}{4} \).

\textit{Step 5: (c) Since \( v = x(x-3)^2 \geq 0 \) for all \( x \in [0,3] \), the velocity is never negative --- the object never reverses direction. Total distance = area under \( v \)-\( t \) graph.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Distance} = \int_0^3 x(x-3)^2\, \mathrm{d}x = \dfrac{27}{4} \text{ m}
\end{gathered}\]
Distance equals the definite integral since \( v \geq 0 \) throughout.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) \(\displaystyle \dfrac{27}{4}\) square units   (c) Object does not reverse as \(\displaystyle v \geq 0\) for \(\displaystyle x \in [0,3]\); distance \(\displaystyle = \dfrac{27}{4}\) m
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 36\quad\small\texttt{(i2-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 36 (i2-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 3x^2 \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int 3x^2 \, \mathrm{d}x = x^3
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule: \( \int 3x^2\,\mathrm{d}x = 3\cdot\dfrac{x^3}{3} = x^3 \). No \( +C \) is needed because the constant cancels in \( F(b) - F(a) \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[x^3]_1^4 = 64 - 1 = 63
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate \( F(4) - F(1) \). Always subtract lower-limit value from upper-limit value --- swapping them would flip the sign.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  63 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 37\quad\small\texttt{(i2-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 37 (i2-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^3 (4x - 1) \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (4x - 1) \, \mathrm{d}x = 2x^2 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule term by term. The constant of integration is dropped since it cancels when we subtract \( F(b) - F(a) \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[2x^2 - x]_0^3 = (18 - 3) - 0 = 15
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate the antiderivative at \( x = 3 \) and \( x = 0 \), then subtract the lower-limit value from the upper.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  15 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 38\quad\small\texttt{(i2-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 38 (i2-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_2^5 6x \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int 6x \, \mathrm{d}x = 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule: \( \int 6x\,\mathrm{d}x = 6\cdot\dfrac{x^2}{2} = 3x^2 \). No \( +C \) needed for a definite integral.

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[3x^2]_2^5 = 75 - 12 = 63
\end{gathered}\]
Compute \( 3(5)^2 - 3(2)^2 = 75 - 12 \). Upper minus lower --- never the other way round.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  63 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 39\quad\small\texttt{(i2-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 39 (i2-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_{-1}^{2} (x^2 + 3) \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (x^2 + 3) \, \mathrm{d}x = \frac{x^3}{3} + 3x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule term by term. The constant of integration cancels in \( F(b) - F(a) \), so we omit it.

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(\frac{8}{3} + 6\right) - \left(-\frac{1}{3} - 3\right) = \frac{26}{3} + \frac{10}{3} = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 2 \) then \( x = -1 \) and subtract. Be careful with signs at the negative limit: \( (-1)^3 = -1 \), and subtracting a negative becomes addition.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  12 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 40\quad\small\texttt{(i2-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 40 (i2-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^1 (5x^4 + 2x) \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int (5x^4 + 2x) \, \mathrm{d}x = x^5 + x^2
\end{gathered}\]
\( 5x^4 \to 5\cdot\dfrac{x^5}{5} = x^5 \) and \( 2x \to x^2 \). No \( +C \) --- it would cancel in the subtraction \( F(b) - F(a) \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[x^5 + x^2]_0^1 = (1 + 1) - 0 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate at \( x = 1 \) then \( x = 0 \). The lower-limit contribution is zero, so the integral equals \( F(1) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  2 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 41\quad\small\texttt{(i2-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 41 (i2-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 2\sqrt{x} \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int 2x^{\frac{1}{2}} \, \mathrm{d}x = \frac{4}{3} x^{\frac{3}{2}}
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2} \), then apply the power rule with \( n = \tfrac{1}{2} \): \( 2\cdot\dfrac{x^{3/2}}{3/2} = \dfrac{4}{3}x^{3/2} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{4}{3}(8) - \frac{4}{3}(1) = \frac{32}{3} - \frac{4}{3} = \frac{28}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^{3/2} = (\sqrt{4})^3 = 8 \) and \( 1^{3/2} = 1 \). Subtract \( F(1) \) from \( F(4) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{28}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 42\quad\small\texttt{(i2-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 42 (i2-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^3 \frac{4}{x^2} \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int 4x^{-2} \, \mathrm{d}x = -\frac{4}{x}
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule with \( n = -2 \): \( 4\cdot\dfrac{x^{-1}}{-1} = -\dfrac{4}{x} \). The \( +C \) is dropped because it cancels in the final subtraction.

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-\frac{4}{3} - (-4) = -\frac{4}{3} + 4 = \frac{8}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Compute \( F(3) - F(1) \). Subtracting a negative becomes addition.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{8}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 43\quad\small\texttt{(i2-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 43 (i2-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the area under \( y = x^2 + 1 \) between \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{x^3}{3} + x\right]_0^3 = 9 + 3 = 12
\end{gathered}\]
The curve \( y = x^2 + 1 \) is positive for all real \( x \), so the definite integral equals the signed area, which here is the actual area. Integrate, then compute \( F(3) - F(0) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  12 \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\end{center}
\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 44\quad\small\texttt{(i2-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 44 (i2-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area between \( y = 6x - x^2 \) and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x - x^2 = 0 \implies x(6-x) = 0 \implies x = 0, 6
\end{gathered}\]
The roots give the bounds of the enclosed region. Between them, the downward-opening parabola lies above the \( x \)-axis, so the integral gives the area directly.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[3x^2 - \frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^6 = 108 - 72 = 36
\end{gathered}\]
A definite integral equals signed area between curve and \( x \)-axis. The curve is above the axis on \([0,6]\), so this value is the actual area.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  36 \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\end{center}
\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 45\quad\small\texttt{(i2-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 45 (i2-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_{-2}^{1} (3x^2 - 2x) \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[x^3 - x^2]_{-2}^{1} = (1 - 1) - (-8 - 4) = 0 + 12 = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Antiderivative is \( x^3 - x^2 \). Substitute upper limit \( x = 1 \) and lower limit \( x = -2 \), then subtract. Take care with \( (-2)^3 = -8 \) --- cubing a negative gives a negative.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  12 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 46\quad\small\texttt{(i2-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 46 (i2-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^9 \frac{3}{\sqrt{x}} \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int 3x^{-\frac{1}{2}} \, \mathrm{d}x = 6\sqrt{x}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite \( \dfrac{3}{\sqrt{x}} = 3x^{-1/2} \), then use the power rule: \( 3\cdot\dfrac{x^{1/2}}{1/2} = 6x^{1/2} = 6\sqrt{x} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6(3) - 6(1) = 12
\end{gathered}\]
\( \sqrt{9} = 3 \) and \( \sqrt{1} = 1 \). Subtract the lower-limit value from the upper.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  12 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 47\quad\small\texttt{(i2-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 47 (i2-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area under \( y = 4 - x^2 \) above the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4 - x^2 = 0 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
The roots \( x = \pm 2 \) are the natural bounds --- between them the parabola is above the \( x \)-axis, so the integral over \([-2, 2]\) gives the area directly without sign correction.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[4x - \frac{x^3}{3}\right]_{-2}^{2} = \frac{16}{3} + \frac{16}{3} = \frac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Compute \( F(2) - F(-2) = \left(8 - \tfrac{8}{3}\right) - \left(-8 + \tfrac{8}{3}\right) = \tfrac{16}{3} + \tfrac{16}{3} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{32}{3} \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 48\quad\small\texttt{(i2-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 48 (i2-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^2 x(x + 3) \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Expand and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{3x^2}{2}\right]_0^2 = \frac{8}{3} + 6 = \frac{26}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Expand \( x(x+3) = x^2 + 3x \), apply the power rule to each term (no \( +C \) needed), then compute \( F(2) - F(0) \). The lower limit gives zero.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{26}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 49\quad\small\texttt{(i2-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 49 (i2-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 \left( x + \frac{1}{x^2} \right) \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Integrate and evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{x^2}{2} - \frac{1}{x}\right]_1^4 = \left(8 - \frac{1}{4}\right) - \left(\frac{1}{2} - 1\right) = \frac{31}{4} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{33}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite \( \dfrac{1}{x^2} = x^{-2} \) so \( \int x^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x = -\dfrac{1}{x} \). Then compute \( F(4) - F(1) \), being careful with signs of the negative-power term.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{33}{4} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 50\quad\small\texttt{(i2-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 50 (i2-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^1 (2x + 1)^2 \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2x+1)^2 = 4x^2 + 4x + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( (a+b)^2 = a^2 + 2ab + b^2 \) before integrating --- the power rule only applies to single powers of \( x \), not to squared binomials.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{4x^3}{3} + 2x^2 + x\right]_0^1 = \frac{4}{3} + 2 + 1 = \frac{13}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Integrate term by term, then compute \( F(1) - F(0) \). The lower-limit contribution is zero.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{13}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 51\quad\small\texttt{(i2-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 51 (i2-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area enclosed between \( y = x^2 - 4x \) and the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(x-4) = 0 \implies x = 0, 4
\end{gathered}\]
The roots give the bounds of the enclosed region. The upward-opening parabola dips below the \( x \)-axis between its roots.

\textit{Step 2: Curve is below axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^4 (x^2-4x)\,\mathrm{d}x = \left[\frac{x^3}{3}-2x^2\right]_0^4 = \frac{64}{3}-32 = -\frac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
A definite integral gives signed area: regions below the \( x \)-axis contribute negatively. The negative value here confirms the curve is below the axis on \([0,4]\).

\textit{Step 3: Area.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \frac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Take the absolute value because area is always positive. If the curve had crossed the axis inside the interval, we would split at the crossing and take \(|\cdot|\) of each piece before adding.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{32}{3} \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 52\quad\small\texttt{(i2-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 52 (i2-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^8 \frac{2}{\sqrt[3]{x}} \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite and integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int 2x^{-\frac{1}{3}}\,\mathrm{d}x = 3x^{\frac{2}{3}}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite \( \dfrac{2}{\sqrt[3]{x}} = 2x^{-1/3} \), then apply the power rule with \( n = -\tfrac{1}{3} \): \( 2\cdot\dfrac{x^{2/3}}{2/3} = 3x^{2/3} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply limits.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3(4) - 3(1) = 9
\end{gathered}\]
\( 8^{\frac{2}{3}} = (\sqrt[3]{8})^2 = 2^2 = 4 \) and \( 1^{2/3} = 1 \). Subtract \( F(1) \) from \( F(8) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  9 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 53\quad\small\texttt{(i2-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 53 (i2-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the area bounded by \( y = \sqrt{x} \), the \( x \)-axis, and \( x = 9 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Integrate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{2}{3}x^{\frac{3}{2}}\right]_0^9 = \frac{2}{3}(27) = 18
\end{gathered}\]
The natural lower bound is \( x = 0 \), where \( y = \sqrt{x} \) meets the \( x \)-axis. The curve is above the axis on \([0,9]\), so the integral gives the area directly. \( 9^{3/2} = (\sqrt{9})^3 = 27 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  18 \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 54\quad\small\texttt{(i2-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 54 (i2-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_{-1}^{3} (x-1)(x+2) \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x-1)(x+2) = x^2 + x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply out the brackets so the power rule can be applied term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{x^3}{3}+\frac{x^2}{2}-2x\right]_{-1}^{3} = \frac{15}{2} - \frac{13}{6} = \frac{16}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
At \( x = 3 \): \( 9 + \tfrac{9}{2} - 6 = \tfrac{15}{2} \). At \( x = -1 \): \( -\tfrac{1}{3} + \tfrac{1}{2} + 2 = \tfrac{13}{6} \). Subtract upper minus lower.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{16}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 55\quad\small\texttt{(i2-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 55 (i2-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 \frac{x^2 + 1}{\sqrt{x}} \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^{\frac{3}{2}} + x^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide each numerator term by \( x^{1/2} \): \( \dfrac{x^2}{x^{1/2}} = x^{3/2} \) and \( \dfrac{1}{x^{1/2}} = x^{-1/2} \). The integrand must be a sum of powers of \( x \) before the power rule can be applied.

\textit{Step 2: Integrate and evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{2}{5}x^{\frac{5}{2}} + 2x^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]_1^4 = \left(\frac{64}{5}+4\right)-\left(\frac{2}{5}+2\right) = \frac{62}{5}+2 = \frac{72}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^{5/2} = (\sqrt{4})^5 = 32 \) and \( 4^{1/2} = 2 \). Compute \( F(4) - F(1) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{72}{5} \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 56\quad\small\texttt{(i2-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 56 (i2-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given that \( \displaystyle\int_0^k 2x \, \mathrm{d}x = 18 \), find \( k \) where \( k > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Integrate and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[x^2]_0^k = k^2 = 18 \implies k = 3\sqrt{2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \int 2x\,\mathrm{d}x = x^2 \); evaluating between 0 and \( k \) gives \( k^2 \). Solve \( k^2 = 18 \) and take the positive root since \( k > 0 \). Simplify the surd: \( \sqrt{18} = 3\sqrt{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  k = 3\sqrt{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 57\quad\small\texttt{(i2-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 57 (i2-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Given that \( \displaystyle\int_1^k 3x^2 \, \mathrm{d}x = 26 \), find \( k \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Integrate and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[x^3]_1^k = k^3 - 1 = 26 \implies k^3 = 27 \implies k = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Antiderivative is \( x^3 \). Evaluating from 1 to \( k \) gives \( k^3 - 1 \) (upper limit minus lower limit --- never reverse the order). Solve the cubic equation in \( k \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  k = 3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 58\quad\small\texttt{(i2-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 58 (i2-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area enclosed between \( y = x^2 \) and \( y = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Intersections.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Equate the two \( y \)-values to find where the curves cross --- these are the limits of the enclosed region.

\textit{Step 2: Top minus bottom.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-2}^{2}(4-x^2)\,\mathrm{d}x = \left[4x-\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_{-2}^{2} = \frac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
For area between two curves, integrate (upper - lower) across the intersection bounds. On \((-2, 2)\), the line \( y = 4 \) lies above the parabola \( y = x^2 \), so we integrate \( 4 - x^2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{32}{3} \) square units
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 59\quad\small\texttt{(i2-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 59 (i2-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area between \( y = x^2 \) and \( y = x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Intersections.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = x \implies x(x-1)=0 \implies x=0,1
\end{gathered}\]
Set the two equations equal to find the \( x \)-values where the curves cross. These give the integration limits.

\textit{Step 2: Top minus bottom.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^1(x-x^2)\,\mathrm{d}x = \left[\frac{x^2}{2}-\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^1 = \frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{3}=\frac{1}{6}
\end{gathered}\]
For \( 0 < x < 1 \), test \( x = \tfrac{1}{2} \): \( y = \tfrac{1}{2} \) on the line and \( \tfrac{1}{4} \) on the parabola, so the line is on top. Integrate (upper - lower) = \( x - x^2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{1}{6} \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 60\quad\small\texttt{(i2-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 60 (i2-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_1^4 \left(\sqrt{x} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\right) \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Integrate and evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{2}{3}x^{\frac{3}{2}}-2x^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]_1^4 = \left(\frac{16}{3}-4\right)-\left(\frac{2}{3}-2\right) = \frac{4}{3}+\frac{4}{3}=\frac{8}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite \( \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2} \) and \( \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{x}} = x^{-1/2} \), then apply the power rule to each. Compute \( F(4) - F(1) \) --- subtracting a negative becomes addition.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{8}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Definite Integration 61\quad\small\texttt{(i2-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 61 (i2-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Show that \( \displaystyle\int_0^2 (3x^2 + 2x + 1) \, \mathrm{d}x = 14 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[x^3+x^2+x]_0^2 = 8+4+2 = 14 \, \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
Integrate term by term (no \( +C \) needed for a definite integral) and compute \( F(2) - F(0) \). The result matches the given value, completing the proof.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown: \(\displaystyle  14 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 62\quad\small\texttt{(i2-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 62 (i2-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area between \( y = x^3 - 4x \) and the \( x \)-axis for \( 0 \le x \le 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Curve is below axis for \( 0 < x < 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^2(x^3-4x)\,\mathrm{d}x = \left[\frac{x^4}{4}-2x^2\right]_0^2 = 4-8 = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Test \( x = 1 \): \( y = 1 - 4 = -3 < 0 \), confirming the curve is below the \( x \)-axis on \((0, 2)\). The integral is therefore negative --- it gives signed area, not actual area.

\textit{Step 2: Area.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Take the absolute value of the integral because area is positive. Note the curve does not cross the axis between 0 and 2, so no splitting is needed.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  4 \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 63\quad\small\texttt{(i2-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 63 (i2-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \displaystyle\int_1^2 \frac{x^4 + x^2}{x^2} \, \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Simplify to \( x^2 + 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{x^3}{3}+x\right]_1^2 = \frac{14}{3}-\frac{4}{3} = \frac{10}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide each numerator term by \( x^2 \): \( \dfrac{x^4 + x^2}{x^2} = x^2 + 1 \). Then integrate and compute \( F(2) - F(1) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{10}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 64\quad\small\texttt{(i2-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 64 (i2-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the area between \( y = 6 - x^2 \) and \( y = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Intersections.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6-x^2=2 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Set the two equations equal to locate where the curve meets the horizontal line. These give the bounds of integration.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-2}^{2}(4-x^2)\,\mathrm{d}x = \frac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
For area between two curves, integrate (upper - lower) between the intersections. On \((-2, 2)\), the parabola \( y = 6 - x^2 \) lies above the line \( y = 2 \), so the integrand is \( (6 - x^2) - 2 = 4 - x^2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{32}{3} \) square units
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Definite Integration 65\quad\small\texttt{(i2-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 65 (i2-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given that \( \displaystyle\int_0^a 3x^2 \, \mathrm{d}x = 8 \), find \( a \) where \( a > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
[x^3]_0^a = a^3 = 8 \implies a = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The antiderivative of \( 3x^2 \) is \( x^3 \); evaluated from 0 to \( a \) it equals \( a^3 \). Solve the cubic, taking the positive root since \( a > 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  a = 2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Definite Integration 66\quad\small\texttt{(i2-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 66 (i2-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the area between \( y = x^2 \) and \( y = 2x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Intersections.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2=2x \implies x(x-2)=0 \implies x=0,2
\end{gathered}\]
Set the two equations equal to find where they cross --- these are the limits of the enclosed region.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_0^2(2x-x^2)\,\mathrm{d}x = \left[x^2-\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^2 = 4-\frac{8}{3}=\frac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
On \((0, 2)\), test \( x = 1 \): \( y = 2 \) on the line and \( y = 1 \) on the parabola, so the line is above. Integrate (upper - lower) = \( 2x - x^2 \) across the bounds.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{4}{3} \) square units
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 67\quad\small\texttt{(i2-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 67 (i2-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Evaluate \( \displaystyle\int_0^4 \left(3\sqrt{x} - 2\right) \mathrm{d}x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[2x^{\frac{3}{2}} - 2x\right]_0^4 = (16 - 8) - 0 = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite \( 3\sqrt{x} = 3x^{1/2} \). Power rule gives \( 3\cdot\dfrac{x^{3/2}}{3/2} = 2x^{3/2} \). At \( x = 4 \): \( 2(8) - 2(4) = 8 \); at \( x = 0 \): \( 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  8 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 68\quad\small\texttt{(i2-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 68 (i2-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find \( \displaystyle\int_{-1}^{2} (x+1)(x-2) \, \mathrm{d}x \) and interpret your answer geometrically.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply out the brackets so the power rule applies term by term. Roots are \( x = -1, 2 \) --- the upward parabola dips below the \( x \)-axis between them.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{x^3}{3}-\frac{x^2}{2}-2x\right]_{-1}^{2} = -\frac{9}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
At \( x = 2 \): \( \tfrac{8}{3} - 2 - 4 = -\tfrac{10}{3} \). At \( x = -1 \): \( -\tfrac{1}{3} - \tfrac{1}{2} + 2 = \tfrac{7}{6} \). Subtract: \( -\tfrac{10}{3} - \tfrac{7}{6} = -\tfrac{27}{6} = -\tfrac{9}{2} \).

\textit{Step 3: Interpretation: the curve is below the \( x \)-axis between the roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Area} = \frac{9}{2} \text{ square units}
\end{gathered}\]
Because the curve is below the \( x \)-axis on \((-1, 2)\), the integral is negative --- it gives signed area. The actual area is the absolute value, \( \tfrac{9}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  -\dfrac{9}{2} \); area \(\displaystyle  = \dfrac{9}{2} \) square units
\end{answerbox}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 69\quad\small\texttt{(i2-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 69 (i2-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the area between \( y = x^2 - 1 \) and \( y = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Intersections.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 1 = 3 \implies x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Set the curve and the horizontal line equal to find where they meet. These give the integration bounds.

\textit{Step 2: Top minus bottom.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\int_{-2}^{2}(4-x^2)\,\mathrm{d}x = \frac{32}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Between intersections, integrate (upper - lower). The line \( y = 3 \) is above the parabola \( y = x^2 - 1 \) on \((-2, 2)\), so the integrand is \( 3 - (x^2 - 1) = 4 - x^2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{32}{3} \) square units
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The integral as the shaded (signed) area between the curve and the \(x\)-axis over the limits.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Definite Integration 70\quad\small\texttt{(i2-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definite Integration 70 (i2-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The region \( R \) is bounded by \( y = x^2 + 2 \), the \( x \)-axis, and the lines \( x = 1 \) and \( x = 3 \). Find the area of \( R \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left[\frac{x^3}{3}+2x\right]_1^3 = 15 - \frac{7}{3} = \frac{38}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve \( y = x^2 + 2 \geq 2 > 0 \), so it lies above the \( x \)-axis throughout \([1, 3]\). The definite integral therefore gives the area directly. Compute \( F(3) - F(1) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{38}{3} \) square units
\end{answerbox}
